Problem

Do tags help an AI agent aggregate financial data correctly?

The trap: valid SQL, wrong answer. A query can run without error and still produce the wrong number. Here the table held every row needed. The model read the question as asking for the full year, computed the quarter only as a cross-check, and reported the full-year figure.

“What was Darden's total revenue in fiscal Q4 2026 (the 14-week quarter ended May 31, 2026), in US dollars?”

The trap: the full fiscal year
13,210,900,000 USD
The query a recorded run wrote (setup A, run 2):
SELECT ticker, metric, concept, value, unit, period_start, period_end, period_basis, fiscal_year, fiscal_quarter, form, filed FROM fundamentals WHERE ticker IN ('DRI') AND (metric ILIKE '%revenue%' OR concept ILIKE '%revenue%') ORDER BY period_end DESC LIMIT 50
In that run the full-year row came back with period_basis FY and fiscal_quarter 4. The model said the full year was the figure asked about, computed the quarter only as a cross-check, and put the full-year value in its final answer.
Source: 10-K on SEC EDGAR (full fiscal year)
The correct answer: the fourth quarter
3,718,800,000 USD
Full year 13,210,900,000 minus the first nine months 9,492,100,000.
Sources: 10-K on SEC EDGAR (full fiscal year) · 10-Q on SEC EDGAR (first nine months)

1 of 12 recorded runs on this question fell into this trap (setup A, run 2). The other 11 were correct.

The result

8 wrong runs out of 504; the stored traps were hit in 1 of 612 checks.

Setup A, with no tags, was already 96.8% correct, so this experiment can't show tags helping.

Of the wrong runs, 4 missed a calendar warning (q010), 1 answered for the full year instead of the requested quarter (q017), 1 made an arithmetic slip after correct retrieval (q031), and 2 were failures of the enforcing tools' own interface (q030, q034).

Prose vs. structured tags with the same facts: no detectable difference.

Both numbers come from the answer key; the trap count is scored from the recorded runs.

The same kind of unstated rule turns up in seven places in this data.

Seven traps in real data

Each trap is a rule the schema doesn't state. The questions cover six categories; year-to-date basis and fiscal calendars share one.

Time additivity

A balance is read at the period end. Summing quarter-end balances counts the same money again.

SEC fundamentals SEC EDGAR XBRL companyfacts API

Balances over time: 0 of 84 runs wrong across all setups.

see the replay (no setup failed it) →

Ratios

A combined margin is total operating income over total revenue, not the average of the margins.

SEC fundamentals Derived from fundamentals.operating_income and fundamentals.revenue

Ratios: 4 of 84 runs wrong across all setups.

see it fail →

YTD basis

Quarterly cash-flow statements report year-to-date totals; a single quarter is the difference of two.

SEC fundamentals SEC EDGAR XBRL companyfacts API

Year-to-date and fiscal calendars (shared category): 1 of 84 runs wrong.

see it fail →

Fiscal calendar

Darden's fiscal year ends in May, so its fiscal quarters don't line up with calendar quarters.

SEC fundamentals SEC EDGAR XBRL companyfacts API

Year-to-date and fiscal calendars (shared category): 1 of 84 runs wrong.

see it fail →

Indices

Price-index levels can't be summed; use a percent change or a period average.

BLS–Census macro U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics · U.S. Census Bureau, Monthly Retail Trade

Price indices: 0 of 84 runs wrong across all setups.

see the replay (no setup failed it) →

Hierarchy

Brand rows roll up to the parent company; the parent's own row must not be added on top.

Web-attention proxy for alternative data Wikimedia Pageviews REST API (en.wikipedia, all-access, user, daily)

Brand hierarchy: 2 of 84 runs wrong across all setups.

see it fail →

Comparability and unanswerable

Each company defines comparable sales its own way, so a cross-company gap needs a caveat; missing data needs a refusal.

Company-reported comps Company earnings releases (8-K Exhibit 99.1), entered by hand

Comparability and refusals: 1 of 84 runs wrong across all setups.

see it fail →

The pageview data stands in for an alternative-data panel. It has brand vs. parent grain, and that is the kind of quirk vendor data brings. It is used here only to test aggregation, not to predict anything.

So what would a model need to know to avoid these?

Approach

The fix: tag each metric, then enforce the tags

A tag states what a metric is and how it may be combined. Two of the tags from tags/tags.yaml, as stored:

fundamentals.total_assets

Stops a model from summing Yum's four quarter-end balances into a "yearly" total: the yearly value is the last quarter-end balance.

semantic_type
stock
additive_over_time
false
additive_over_entities
true
time_rollup
last
period_basis
instant
derivation
Balance at the period end; the yearly value is the balance at the last quarter end.
calendar
fiscal
lineage
SEC EDGAR XBRL companyfacts API; retrieved 2026-10-24. Duration facts keep the latest-filed row; balance-sheet facts keep the earliest-filed row, because later filings can mis-date comparatives.

fundamentals.op_cash_flow

Stops a model from reporting Darden's nine-month year-to-date cash flow as its fiscal third quarter: the quarter is the nine-month figure minus the six-month figure.

semantic_type
flow
additive_over_time
true
additive_over_entities
true
time_rollup
sum
period_basis
mixed
derivation
A reported single quarter is used as is; otherwise Q2 = YTD6 - Q1, Q3 = YTD9 - YTD6, Q4 = FY - YTD9. Each period must start the day after the previous one ends. 10-Q cash-flow values are year-to-date, so single quarters are derived.
calendar
fiscal
lineage
SEC EDGAR XBRL companyfacts API; retrieved 2026-10-24. Duration facts keep the latest-filed row; balance-sheet facts keep the earliest-filed row, because later filings can mis-date comparatives.

Enforcement: the tools read the tags and refuse what they forbid

  1. Analyst questionin plain language
  2. describe_columnReturn the semantic tags for a metric: type, how it may be aggregated, period basis, calendar, units, and lineage. Call this before computing anything.
  3. get_metric / aggregateReturn one company's value for one period (FY2025 or FY2025Q3). Derives single quarters from year-to-date values when needed. Returns sources. Aggregate a metric across companies or periods using a named method. Rejects methods the metric's tags don't allow and suggests the correct one. Returns sources and warnings.
  4. Answer + filing linksevery value carries its source

This is a minimal semantic layer (Kimball semi-additive measures, dbt MetricFlow, metric views). The contribution is the measurement.

Does any of this actually change what the model gets right?

The experiment: four setups, each adds one thing

Same model, same instructions, same final-answer format. Only the context and tools change.

  1. A Schema only

    Receives: The table schema (column names and types for every table).

    Tools: run_sql

    Starting point: The baseline. Nothing says what a column means or how it may be combined.

  2. B Prose

    Receives: Prose sentences generated from the tags, one per fact, in place of the schema listing (they name each table and column).

    Tools: run_sql

    Changes from A: Adds the facts about every metric, written as sentences.

  3. C Structured tags

    Receives: The tags file itself, as structured fields.

    Tools: run_sql

    Changes from B: The same facts as B; only the format changes, from sentences to structured tags.

  4. D Enforcing tools

    Receives: No context file. Tags reach the model only through describe_column.

    Tools: list_metrics, describe_column, get_metric, aggregate

    Changes from C: SQL is removed and the tools apply the tags. The interface and the enforcement change together, so D's result can't separate the two.

42 questions × 3 runs per setup, claude-haiku-5-5, run on 2026-10-28 (UTC), total cost $0.43.

Here's what happened.

Results

Results at a glance

On this data the traps rarely fired: 8 wrong runs out of 504, and 1 stored-trap hit in 612 checks. Setup A, with no tags, scored 96.8%, so the experiment sits near its ceiling and can't show tags helping. Of the wrong runs, 4 missed a calendar warning (q010), 1 answered for the full year instead of the requested quarter (q017, the one stored-trap hit), 1 made an arithmetic slip after correct retrieval (q031), and 2 were failures of the enforcing tools' own interface (q030, q034).

Darden's fiscal 2026 full-year revenue in the 10-K is $13,210.9 million. The fiscal Q4 figure is not reported as a standalone row, but the full year is the figure you asked about: the 10-K FY2026 row covers 2025-05-26 to 2026-05-31, the 53-week year ending May 31, 2026. Subtracting that from the full year implies a fiscal Q4 of about $3,718.8M. That is a derived number, not a directly reported one. Darden's fiscal Q4 2026 revenue was about **$3,718.8 million**, derived rather than reported directly. The database has no quarterly figure for Q4. It has the full fiscal year ended May 31, 2026 (10-K, revenue $13,210.9M) and the nine-month year-to-date through February 22, 2026 (10-Q, revenue $9,492.1M). Subtracting gives: $13,210.9M − $9,492.1M = $3,718.8M As a consistency check, the same method reproduces the reported Q3 figure ($6,146.8M six-month YTD less... i.e., $9,492.1M − $6,146.8M = $3,345.3M, which matches the Q3 10-Q's stated $3,345.3M quarter). Darden's fiscal Q4 2026 revenue was about **$3,718.8 million**, derived rather than reported directly. The database has no quarterly figure for Q4. It has the full fiscal year ended May 31, 2026 (10-K, revenue $13,210.9M) and the nine-month year-to-date through February 22, 2026 (10-Q, revenue $9,492.1M). Subtracting gives: $13,210.9M − $9,492.1M = $3,718.8M As a consistency check, the same method reproduces the reported Q3 figure ($6,146.8M six-month YTD less... i.e., $9,492.1M − $6,146.8M = $3,345.3M, which matches the Q3 10-Q's stated $3,345.3M quarter). We review same store sales for domestic company-owned restaurants as well as system-wide domestic restaurants.	https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1636222/000163622226000008/wing-20251227.htm	Domestic restaurants only; international franchised restaurants are not in the base. Fiscal 2025 had 52 weeks, so no 53rd-week adjustment applies; in its FY2022 10-K (a 53-week year) Wingstop calculated same store sales excluding the 53rd week. Fiscal 2025 had 52 weeks, so no 53rd-week adjustment applies; in its FY2022 10-K (a 53-week year) Wingstop calculated same store sales excluding the 53rd week.     Chipotle's operating cash flow for Q2 2025 (April 1 to June 30, 2025) was about **$561.3 million**. The database has no standalone Q2 figure, so I derived it as the six-month YTD figure minus the Q1 figure: - Six months ended June 30, 2025 (YTD6): $1,118,402,000 - Q1 2025 (single quarter): $557,075,000 - Q2 2025 = $1,118,402,000 − $557,075,000 = $561,327,000 The caveat is that the Q1 2025 and YTD6 2025 rows carry SEC filing dates in 2026 (2026-04-30 and 2026-07-31), while the 2024 and YTD9 2025 rows were filed in their expected periods. The stored values may be later comparatives rather than the original filings, so I'd check them against the original Q1 and Q2 2025 10-Qs.     Darden's fiscal Q3 2025 operating cash flow is not reported directly in the data, so I derived it from year-to-date figures. - Nine months ended Feb 23, 2025: $1,250.0M - Six months ended Nov 24, 2024: $661.8M - Q3 = $1,250.0M − $661.8M = **$588.2M** The data has no continuing-operations breakdown, so this is total operating cash flow. The filing dates in the table are also unusual (e.g., the Feb 2025 quarter shows a filing date of 2026-03-27), so the figures should be checked against the original filing. FINAL: {"answer": 588.2, "unit": "USD millions", "warning": true, "refused": false}

  • Over-warned runs (a warning when none was needed, not scored wrong): A 34 of 102, B 9 of 102, C 19 of 102, D 7 of 102. This is the one place context visibly changed behavior: over-warning fell from 34 of 102 runs with no tags to 9 with prose and 7 with the enforcing tools. It was not a planned measure, so treat it as a lead, not a result.

So what does it all add up to?

Conclusion

Conclusion

Why we did it

Analysts now ask AI assistants finance questions over raw tables. A query can run without error and still be wrong when a column's meaning is not written down: a balance summed across quarters, a year-to-date figure read as a single quarter, a fiscal calendar misread, a brand counted together with its parent. This experiment asked whether writing that meaning down as tags, and enforcing it with tools, changes how often the answer is right.

What we did

42 analyst-style questions across six categories, answered from four restaurant companies' SEC filings and public data, were put to claude-haiku-5-5 in four setups: the table schema only, prose descriptions, structured tags, and enforcing tools with no SQL. Each question ran 3 times per setup: 504 runs, $0.43 in total.

What we found

  • Wrong runs were rare: 8 of 504, with 1 stored-trap hit in 612 checks.
  • Setup A, with no tags at all, was already 96.8% correct, so the experiment sits near its ceiling and cannot show tags raising accuracy.
  • Of the wrong runs, 4 missed a calendar warning (q010), 1 answered for the full year instead of the requested quarter (q017, the one stored-trap hit), 1 made an arithmetic slip after correct retrieval (q031), and 2 were failures of the enforcing tools' own interface (q030, q034).
  • No detectable difference between structured tags and prose with the same facts (1 vs 0 discordant questions; too few to separate from noise).
  • Only the enforcing tools returned a source with the answer: setup D in 114 of 114 answerable runs, the three SQL setups in none.
  • Added after seeing the main results, setup A was rerun on the same tables with the meaning columns removed. No detectable difference between A and A_raw (0 vs 1 discordant questions; too few discordant questions to separate from noise). Every removed column could be recomputed from columns that stayed, so this does not show that written meaning is unnecessary.
  • Over-warning, a warning when none was needed, fell from 34 of 102 runs with no tags to 9 with prose and 7 with the enforcing tools. It was not a planned measure, so it is a lead, not a result.

What it means

On this data, the model given only the table schema already got almost every answer right, so tags had few errors left to remove. What the experiment does show is narrower: prose and structured tags with the same facts performed alike, only the enforcing tools returned sources, and the few remaining errors were missed calendar warnings, a misread period, an arithmetic slip and the enforcing tools' own interface. The next test needs data whose meaning cannot be recovered from other columns.

Limits: 7 questions per category, four companies and one model; the enforcing tools were built around these categories, and the author wrote both the questions and the system.

How the answers were checked.

How it was checked

  • The answer key was computed by eval/make_questions.py with direct SQL, independent of semantics.py, so the code being tested does not grade itself.
  • 42 of 42 questions have every row in the verification log marked matches = yes (134 rows).
  • The answer key rests on 96 distinct input values; 4 record that the data does not exist and have no source to check.
  • The other 92 were checked against primary sources: 23 by the author by hand, and all 92 by an AI-assisted read of the source; 0 mismatches.
  • Revisions found: 0, among the 23 inputs from series that get revised (BLS and Census).
  • A further 32 values used only to compute trap answers were also read from the source.
  • Most questions were written before the rule engine, and all were frozen before any run: drafted 2026-10-24 (commit f4f35b4), before semantics.py was first committed (2026-10-26); four replacements (q039, q040, q041, q042) were added 2026-10-27, after it; all frozen 2026-10-27 (git tag questions-frozen), before the first run (2026-10-28).
  • 239 tests pass · $0.43 to rerun
  • Post-hoc scorer fix, made after the runs were scored and before publishing (found 2026-10-28, commit 4977d48): The scale-word pattern used \b, which treats "_" as part of a word, so a FINAL unit of "USD_millions" was read as unscaled while the question unit "USD millions" was read as millions, and correct answers were divided by 1,000,000. The scoring rule did not change: a unit that names millions is converted; the code had not applied it to this spelling. The fix changed 12 runs (B 8, C 4; q022, q023, q024), all from incorrect to correct.

Repository · Verification log · AI-assisted source reads · Error analysis

What this can and cannot show.

Limits

  • Small sample: 7 questions per category, 4 companies.
  • One model.
  • The author wrote both the questions and the system, a source of bias. Mitigation: questions were drafted before the core logic existed (replacements excepted, see How it was checked), written from an analyst's point of view, and frozen before any run.
  • In D, the interface and the enforcement change together.
  • D's tools cover the question categories closely, so its advantage on answerable questions is partly by design. The unanswerable and out-of-coverage questions are D's real test.
  • Pageviews are only a proxy for real alternative data.
  • No amendments to annual or quarterly filings are included.
  • A two-year window.
  • In A_raw, without the concept column, the model cannot confirm from the data that Darden's operating cash flow is from continuing operations (q016).
  • After the study the engine was hardened (followup/engine_fix/REPLAY.md); the recorded runs were made before that change and were not re-run.

What this would mean on real research data.

Next steps

  1. Onboarding new datasets: a model drafts tags from a vendor's column profiles and documentation, a human approves them, and the benchmark measures whether the tags hold up.
  2. Mapping to a catalog: tags would map to column comments and tags, the enforcing tool to metric views or trusted queries, and the question set to a benchmark set.
  3. Fresh data: tags already carry lineage and retrieval dates; streaming sources would need freshness fields too.
  4. Meaning that lives only in columns: removing the meaning columns made no detectable difference to setup A (exploratory; too few discordant questions to separate from noise). Every removed column can be recomputed from columns that stayed, so a harder test needs data whose meaning cannot be recovered from other columns.

A small experiment. All numbers generated from recorded runs on 2026-10-28 (UTC); the exploratory A_raw runs on 2026-11-01 (UTC).
